« Thy teeth as flocks of sheep that are shorn, which 315Dcome up from the washing. » As by the hair of the Church the innumerable multitude of the simple faithful is understood, so also by the teeth the teachers of the Church are understood: for with the teeth we grind our food. And the holy teachers are well conformed to teeth, because the spiritual foods which the simple cannot grasp, they in a certain manner break up small by expounding. Peter especially was the teeth of the Church, to whom it was said in the vision: « Arise, kill, and eat. » As if it were said: Those whom thou convertest to the faith, kill from that which they are, that they may cease to be sinners; and eat, that is, transform them into the body of the Church. And well are these teeth compared to sheep shorn and washed in the washing. For sheep are wont after shearing to be washed in running water, 316Alest the unwashed sweat cling to the body, and generate the scab. For shorn are the holy teachers and masters of the Church, who are both washed in the washing of baptism; and have lost their fleeces, that is, their faculties and substances, for Christ. To all the faithful indeed suits that which he says: « Which come up from the washing, » for no one can be faithful without the washing of baptism. But shearing does not suit all, because not all are so perfect that they are able to lose their goods for Christ, but this especially befits those who, according to the word of the Lord, sell all their goods, and give to the poor, and naked and unencumbered follow Christ. Such, therefore, are the teeth of the holy Church, that is, its preachers and masters, sheep washed in baptism through innocence, rejoicing in voluntary poverty 316Band in the surrender of their goods. There follows: « All with twins, and there is none barren among them. » Because they are preeminent in the twin charity and love of God and of the neighbor, and teach that this be observed, or also because they preach faith and work. And there is none barren among them, namely no one estranged from good works.

« Thy lips are as a scarlet fillet, and thy speech is sweet. » By the lips of the Church the holy preachers are received, who are also figured by the teeth. But scarlet has the appearance of blood or of fire. The lips of the Church, therefore (that is, the holy preachers), are compared to a scarlet fillet, because they assiduously preach the passion of Christ, who for our redemption poured out his blood; or they are likened to a scarlet fillet, because the preachers burn with love, loving those 316Cto whom they impart words, and also those from whom they suffer evils. To this too it pertains that the Holy Spirit appeared in fiery tongues, because he made the apostles both speaking and burning, burning with love, speaking with preaching. And well is it called a scarlet fillet. For a fillet binds and constrains the hair, and the holy teachers by their preaching bind together the multitude of the faithful in the Church unto the unity of faith and love, and reduce them into one comeliness. By the hair can also be received the subtle thoughts of the mind. With a scarlet fillet, therefore, we bind the hairs, when we constrain and curb our thoughts with the discipline of the fear of God. « And thy speech is sweet. » Sweet is the speech of the Church, when the holy teachers either recall to memory the passion of their Redeemer, 316Dand preach how greatly God loved man, or also when they announce the heavenly rewards to their hearers. « Thy cheeks are as a piece of a pomegranate, besides that which lieth hid within. » By the cheeks we said above that the Church is figured. For when we are ashamed, the redness of blood is poured out in the cheeks. But the pomegranate has a reddish rind, while within it is full of a multitude of seeds: whence too it is called the seeded apple. By the piece of a pomegranate, therefore, is received the memory of that same Lord's passion, which is also figured above in the scarlet. For one thing is wont to be expressed by diverse figures. The cheeks of the Church, therefore, are compared to a piece of a pomegranate, because the holy teachers reverently keep the memory of the passion 317Aof their Redeemer, nor are they ashamed of his cross, nay, they glory, saying with the Apostle: « But God forbid that I should glory, save in the cross of our Lord Jesus Christ; » and elsewhere the Apostle, writing to his disciple: « Be not thou (he says) ashamed of the cross of Christ, nor of me his prisoner. » And well are the cheeks compared not to a whole pomegranate, but to a piece. For the pomegranate, when it is broken, shows redness without, whiteness within. So too the holy preachers, when they announce the passion of Christ, are as it were red without; but when they demonstrate how great a profit of our redemption has come thence, and that man through the passion of Christ has merited not only to be justified from sins, but even to be admitted to the divine fellowship, it is as it were white which lies hidden within. There can 317Balso by the cheeks of the Church be figured the martyrs, who are reddened by the shedding of their blood, like a pomegranate. But the pomegranate being broken, whiteness appears within, because after death they shine with miracles.

« Thy neck is as the tower of David, which is built with bulwarks. » The neck of the holy Church is its holy preachers and teachers. For the same are here designated by the neck who above were designated by the eyes or by the teeth; namely the holy preachers, who, like necks, transmit to the body, for its nourishing, the food of the word received, and utter forth the voice of divine preaching to their hearers. Or also the holy teachers are marked by the neck, because as through the neck the head and the other members are joined to the body, so the holy teachers are as it were mediators between Christ and the rest 317Cof the faithful whom they instruct, and as it were join Christ to his body, that is, to the Church. And this neck is compared to the tower of David. David, who is interpreted "strong of hand" or "desirable in appearance," signifies Christ. And the whole Church indeed is the city of David, that is, of the great king Christ, who is strong of hand, since he conquered the devil, according to what the Psalmist says: « The Lord strong and mighty, the Lord mighty in battle. » He is also desirable in appearance, because (as the Apostle says) the angels desire to look upon him. But the tower of this city are those who are preeminent above the rest either in knowledge or in the perfection of work. « Which is built with bulwarks. » The bulwarks are the divine mysteries of the holy Scriptures, from which, as it were darts, proceed the shafts by which the adversary 317Dpowers are driven back. « A thousand bucklers hang upon it, all the armor of valiant men. » By the thousand bucklers are understood the innumerable defenses of the divine protection, with which the holy Church is walled about and defended. « All the armor of valiant men, » that is, the equipment either of holy work or of divine preaching. And well is the neck of the Church, that is, its preachers and teachers, compared to the tower of David, because they are always as it were in war, fighting for the defense of the holy Church.

« Thy two breasts are like two young roes that are twins. » The two breasts of the Church signify the same holy teachers. Nor is it a wonder that one and the same thing is figured in diverse ways. For the holy teachers are eyes, because the ways of life which they ought to enter upon they 318Ademonstrate to those subject to them; they are teeth, because they grind and break up small the foods of the divine Scriptures, that they may be chewed; they are the neck, because they join the head and the members, that is, Christ and the Church. These same teachers, therefore, are also the breasts of the Church, because they nourish the little ones and the simple faithful with the milk of the word of God, according to what the Apostle, speaking to the more simple, says: « I could not speak to you as unto spiritual, but as unto carnal, and as unto little ones in Christ I gave you milk to drink, not meat. » And well he says not simply "breasts," but with an addition, « two breasts, » since a woman has neither more nor less than two breasts, because assuredly the holy teachers nourish the little ones from either people, namely the Jewish and the Gentile, with the milk of the word of God. For Paul too says of himself: « He who wrought in Peter 318Bto the apostleship of the circumcision, wrought in me also among the Gentiles. » And fitly are the two breasts said to be as two young twin roes, because namely they are the offspring and the sons of our Savior Jesus Christ, who above was designated by the roes: for these animals both see most keenly, and with exceeding swiftness ascend the higher places. So too the teachers of Christ both themselves see what is to be done, and show it to others, and, despising earthly things, strive toward the heavenly. « Who feed among the lilies, » that is, they are delighted with the purest and brightest senses of the Scriptures.

« Till the day break, » that is, till that eternal day come which the Psalmist desired, saying: « Better is one day in thy courts above thousands; » « and the shadows incline, » that is, till the sorrows and perturbations 318Cof this world pass away. « Till the day break, » that is, till the day arise. For the day shall appear, that is, shall arise, when that true sun of justice, that is, Christ, shall appear; and then the shadows shall incline, that is, all the clouds of sorrow and the darkness of the present world shall pass away. But if we wish also to look upon the letter itself, he has beautifully expressed the rising of day and of night. For night is nothing else but the shadow of the earth, which rises up when the sun goes down. But when the sun again ascends above the lands, the shadows incline, that is, night sinks away. They feed, therefore, among the lilies till the day break and the shadows incline, because the preaching and teaching of the holy teachers is necessary only in the present life: for in the other life, after the eternal day shall have appeared and the night of the present world shall have ended, it will 318Dnot be necessary that anyone be taught, because all, the face being revealed, shall contemplate the glory of the Lord. Whence too the Lord says through the Prophet: A man shall not teach his brother, saying, Know the Lord; for all shall know me from the least even to the greatest. « I will go to the mountain of myrrh, and to the hill of frankincense. » In the myrrh, the mortification of the flesh, or the endurance of sufferings for Christ; but in the frankincense, the devotion of holy prayers is received. The mountain of myrrh, therefore, and the hill of frankincense are the minds of the saints, lofty through contemplation. The bridegroom therefore promises that he will come to the mountain of myrrh, and to the hill of frankincense, because he deigns to visit with his visitation those minds which mortify their members with the vices and desires, 319Awhich also make of themselves, through the pursuits of holy prayers, a sacrifice pleasing to God.

Thou art all fair, my love, and there is no spot in thee. » The voice of the bridegroom to his love the bride. « Thou art all fair. » In every part (he says) thou receivest no spot nor wrinkle. For not only the teachers or all the perfect are fair before the eyes of God, who are preeminent in the highest knowledge and contemplation, but also all the simple faithful, who are adorned with right faith and good works, are held fair in the eyes of the bridegroom. For as the Psalmist says, « he hath blessed all that fear the Lord, both little with the great; » therefore he says: « Thou art all fair, » not a part of thee. This we say not because anyone in the present life can be without sin, since it is written: « There is no one upon 319Bthe earth without sin, not even an infant of one day; » but for this reason, because perfect faith and heavenly desire wipe away every spot of lighter sin: for it is not now a question of grave sins, which they who commit are no longer the bride of Christ, nor have any particle of this holy beauty. Hence it is that John says: He who is born of God sinneth not, supply: with a sin unto death, not that anyone is free from light sin, without which this life is not led. All fair, therefore, is the Church, insofar as she keeps herself chaste and free from all sin. But if at any time she is darkened with a light sin, by swift penitence and right faith the ancient beauty is repaired in her unto heavenly desire.

« Come from Lebanon, my bride, come from Lebanon, 319Ccome. » Lebanon is a mountain of Phoenicia. Now from the Hebrew it is interpreted as a whitening or a making-white, but from the Greek it is interpreted as frankincense. Whence also above, where he speaks: « I will go to the mountain of myrrh and to the hills of frankincense, » a hill of Lebanon is meant. Therefore the bridegroom calls his bride, made white by baptism, whitened with the brightness of all the virtues, glowing with zeal for holy prayers. He calls her that she may come, that is, that she may advance in the virtues. God calls the soul not only when he leads it, stripped of the bonds of the flesh, to the heavenly rewards, but he also exhorts it by inward inspiration to advance in the virtues. For as many steps as the saints advance in the virtues, by so many steps, as it were, they press on toward God; and he says « come » three times, because he wills her to be perfect in thought, in speech, and in deed. Or else 319Dhe calls her first, that she may come to him through faith; he calls her a second time, that, now freed from the body, she may receive a worthy recompense of the heavenly rewards; he calls her a third time, that on the day of the general resurrection, the body now taken up again, she may rejoice, adorned forever with a twofold robe. « Thou shalt be crowned from the head of Amana, from the top of Sanir and Hermon, from the dens of lions, from the mountains of leopards. » Amana, they say, is that mountain of Cilicia called Amanus, which is also called Taurus. But Sanir and Hermon are mountains of Judaea, on which lions and leopards are said to dwell. By these mountains, however, are understood the powers of the world, namely kings and princes, who like mountains are lifted up in pride, and who provide dens for the malign spirits as though for lions and leopards. 320AFor the malign spirits are called lions on account of their pride, leopards on account of their ferocity, or also on account of their thousand arts of harming, since the leopard is a spotted animal. From these mountains, therefore, the Church is crowned, when the princes of the world are converted to the faith of Christ, and the Church is rewarded not only for her own virtues, but also for the conversion and salvation of such men. This too can be taken in the present: for the Church is crowned from the top of these mountains, when, the princes being made subject to the catholic faith, the Church, which before was pressed down, is crowned and glories in Christ, as was done in the time of Constantine, when, upon his conversion, the Church was marvelously glorified. She is crowned too from the dens of lions and the mountains of leopards, when she converts the proud and all the savage and crafty, 320Band receives eternal rewards for their conversion also.

« Thou hast wounded my heart, my sister, my bride, thou hast wounded my heart. » His bride and his sister he calls the holy Church, who is both bride, because Christ incarnate betrothed her to himself, and, cleansing her from the filth of sins, joined her to himself with the dowry of the Holy Spirit; and she is sister, because, being incarnate for her sake, he deigned to become her brother, as, rising from the dead, he said to the women: « Go, announce to my brethren. » When therefore he says that he has his heart wounded, he expressed the greatness of the love with which he loves his holy Church. And he affirms this by repeating and pressing it, « Thou hast wounded my heart, » that by such repetition he may show how greatly he loves his Church. But by what 320Cthing it has been wounded he shows by adding: « In one of thine eyes, and in one hair of thy neck. » By the eyes of the Church we said above that the spiritual senses or the holy teachers are to be understood. But by the hairs, the multitudes of the subject peoples, by whom the holy Church is adorned. And the sense is: Though thou art wholly beautiful, O Church, and though I love thee for many things, yet chiefly the brightness of thy spiritual senses and the sincerity of thy teachers delights me; the simplicity too of the subject peoples, who piously and faithfully obey the words of the teachers. And rightly, whereas above he said in the plural eyes and hairs, here he says in the singular in one eye and in one hair, that by this the unity of the holy teachers may be expressed. For the unity of heavenly doctrine is that which they bestow upon their subjects. « For there is one 320DLord, as the Apostle says, one faith, one baptism, » and likewise one devotion of the subject faithful. This too which he says, « thou hast wounded my heart, » can be taken of the passion of Christ: » Thou hast wounded my heart, that is, by thy love thou hast brought it about that I should be wounded on the cross. But it is to be noted that he says, « in one hair of thy neck »: for by the neck we said the teachers are signified, and the hair of the Church clings to the neck, whereby the subject peoples are nourished by the holy teachers, and cling to their sound doctrine, and strive to live according to their precepts.

« How beautiful are thy breasts, my sister, my bride. » By the breasts we said above that these same teachers are figured, who are also designated by the eyes; for 321Athe holy teachers are breasts, that is, they nourish the humble with simple doctrine; they are eyes, when they preach all the highest and heavenly things. He says therefore: « How beautiful are thy breasts, my sister, my bride; that is, how beautiful are thy teachers, since their word both excels in eloquence and is precious for the weight of its meanings. « Thy breasts are more beautiful than wine. » This little verse has already been expounded above, where it is said: « Thy breasts are better than wine. » And this is to be noted, that above the Church praised the breasts of her bridegroom, saying: « Thy breasts are better than wine, » but now in turn the bridegroom extols the breasts of his bride, that by this the unity of Christ and the Church may be commended. For Christ is the head, and the Church his body: for the beginning of the Gospel preaching surpasses the sentence of the Law. « And the odor of thy ointments above 321Ball aromatics. » The ointments of the Church are the charisms of heavenly doctrine, or also the fame of the virtues which is fragrant from the Church; but by the aromatics are understood the observances of the Law. Above the aromatics, then, is the odor of the ointments of the Church, because the fame of the Gospel perfection transcends all praise of the observance of the Law.

« Thy lips are a dripping honeycomb, O bride. » A honeycomb is honey in the wax. Now honey in the wax is spiritual understanding within the letter. The lips of the Church, therefore, are called a dripping honeycomb, because the holy teachers, who are designated by the lips, bring forth spiritual instructions for the faithful who are to be taught. And not only a honeycomb, but a dripping one are the lips of the Church called. For the honeycomb drips, when 321Cthe abundance of honey overflowing can no longer be contained in the waxen cells. Rightly, therefore, is Holy Scripture called a dripping honeycomb, because it is understood in manifold ways and expounded in various senses, now according to the letter, now according to allegory, now according to morality, now according to anagogy, that is, the higher sense. « Honey and milk are under thy tongue. » Milk befits little ones, and therefore by milk is designated the simple doctrine with which the beginning of believers is imbued; but honey, which is believed to be made from the dew of heaven, signifies the heavenly and spiritual doctrine which befits the perfect and the instructed. Under the tongue of the Church, then, are honey and milk, because sometimes they announce the heavenly mysteries to the perfect, sometimes plain and simple things to the untrained. « And the odor of thy garments is like the odor of frankincense. » The garments of the Church are the ornaments of good 321Dworks, according to what John says in the Apocalypse: « For the fine linen is the justifications of the saints. » And in Job it is said: « I was clothed with justice, and I clothed myself as with a robe and a diadem, with my judgment. » But by frankincense we said the purity of holy prayers is designated. The garments of the Church, then, are compared to frankincense, because all the works of the Church are as it were prayers. For in all that she does she always entreats the Lord, and in such manner is fulfilled that which the Lord says: « We ought always to pray. » Nor can this be fulfilled before the Lord in any other way, unless our whole life and conversation be such that it may be reckoned as prayer in the sight of God. Frankincense is a tree of Arabia, in bark and leaf like the laurel, sending forth a juice after the manner of the almond, 322Awhich is gathered twice in the year, in autumn and in spring. In the autumn gathering the trees are prepared, the bark being cut in the most burning heat of summer, and thus, the rich foam leaping forth, which when hardened is thickened, where the nature of the place demands, a palm mat catching it. What has clung to the tree is scraped off with iron, and therefore what is of the bark seems to be the purest, that is, the white frankincense. The second vintage is in spring, the bark being cut for it in winter; this comes out red, and not to be compared with the former. That of the young tree is believed to be whiter, but that of the old more fragrant. What has hung from it in the roundness of a drop, we call male; the drop struck off by a blow, we call manna.

« An enclosed garden is my sister, my bride, an enclosed garden, a sealed fountain. » His sister and bride he calls 322Bthe Church, whom from a handmaid he established to be his sister, and, pledged with the dowry of the Holy Spirit, he made his bride. This Church, therefore, is a garden, because she brings forth the shoots of spiritual virtues, which are designated in what follows by the names of aromatics. And this garden is enclosed, because the holy Church is fortified by the help of her Redeemer and Lord, and walled about with the protection of the angelic powers, and lies open to no snares of the malign spirits. This same Church is a sealed fountain. A fountain, because it flows with the streams of heavenly doctrine, whereby it washes all who believe in Christ from their sins, and gives them the knowledge of truth to drink. And this fountain is sealed, because it is fortified with the seal of Gospel truth, so that neither heretics nor malign spirits are able to violate or break into the catholic faith. 322CThis garden, or this fountain (that is, the Church), was first planted or sprung up in a small little place of Judaea, and then, preaching being disseminated throughout the whole world, sent forth its beds or its little streams. Whence follows:

« Thy shoots are a paradise of pomegranates with the fruits of the orchard. » The shoots of the Church (as has been said) are the increases of faith and the dissemination of preaching. And these shoots are a paradise. Paradise in Greek is garden in Latin, because that primitive Church, which in Judaea was as it were a garden, sent forth throughout the whole world many gardens, that is, Churches. But the pomegranates, which have a ruddy bark, signify those who were not only 322Dsanctified by the general baptism, but also, baptized in their own blood in martyrdom, were made martyrs. But the fruits of the orchard signify the works of the virtues, or those who exercise the virtues themselves in deed. The shoots of the Church, then, were a paradise of pomegranates, because after the apostles the martyrs obtained the first place in the Church, who strove to imitate the very passion of the Lord by suffering; although generally the whole multitude may be taken as a paradise of pomegranates, who are pomegranates because they always retain in memory the passion of their Redeemer. And just as the pomegranate contains within one bark a multitude of seeds, so in the Church the multitude of the faithful is contained within the faith of the Lord's passion. Hence 323Athe Apostle says: « As many of you as have been baptized in Christ Jesus, have been baptized in his death. »

« Cypress with spikenard, spikenard and saffron, sweet cane and cinnamon with all the trees of Lebanon. » Cypress is an aromatic tree, having a seed like coriander, that is, white and somewhat translucent, which is boiled in oil, and thence is pressed out that which is called cyprus, from which a royal ointment is prepared. We read in Exodus that the manna was like the white seed of coriander. Cypress, therefore, and manna have one signification. For they signify heavenly grace, coming, as it were, like manna from the heavenly places. Cypress, then, grows in the garden of the Church, when all the faithful are taught to prefer heavenly grace above all things, and to hope for salvation through it. For no one can be saved by his own virtues, but only by the grace 323Bof God. Spikenard is a type of the Lord's passion. Whence also Mary anointed the head and feet of Jesus with genuine spikenard. Spikenard, then, is in the garden of the Church, when all the saints venerate the memory of the Lord's passion, and give him thanks that he so loved them that he took upon himself death for their salvation and deliverance. « Spikenard and saffron. » Saffron has a flower of golden color, and therefore signifies charity. For as gold is more precious among all metals, so charity holds the primacy among all the virtues, the Apostle saying: « But now there remain faith, hope, charity; but the greater of these is charity. » With spikenard, then, saffron grows in the garden of the Church, when the members of Christ, that is, all the faithful, strive effectually to hold the love of God and of neighbor. Now saffron 323Cis said to cool fiery fevers; so true charity cools the ardor of worldly concupiscence, and kindles in the mind the love of God and of neighbor. » Sweet cane and cinnamon. » The sweet cane is a short shrub, which is also called casia, with a purple bark. It signifies the saints in the Church who are eminent in humility and patience, namely the poor in spirit, whose is the kingdom of heaven. And the purple bark signifies the likeness of the passion of Christ, which those who are grounded in true humility, ever retaining it in their heart, strive to imitate. Cinnamon too is itself a short shrub, but of wondrous virtue and odor, and therefore they designate the progress of humility, which is designated also by the sweet cane. The sweet cane, then, and cinnamon are in the Church, when the saints of God truly hold humility and patience 323Din their heart, and outwardly show them to others. But that the sweet cane has a ruddy bark, while cinnamon has an ashen color, signifies that the saints of God, the more they are mindful of the passion of their Redeemer, the more base and contemptible they are in their own eyes, saying with Abraham: « I will speak to my Lord, though I am dust and ashes; » and with Job: « Therefore I reprove myself, and do penance in ember and ash. » « With all the trees of Lebanon. » The trees of Lebanon are eminent in beauty and height and strength, and therefore signify the teachers and all the perfect in the Church, of whom above the house of the Church is read to be made, the same Church saying: The beams of our houses are of cedar, 324Aour ceilings of cypress, » because, namely, the holy teachers fortify the Church by their preaching. With the sweet cane, then, and cinnamon all the trees of Lebanon are mentioned in the garden of the Church, because whether the humble and patient, or all the perfect teachers, have the same faith in the Church, and await the same beatitude. « Myrrh and aloe with all the chief ointments. » Myrrh is a tree whose juice is called stacte, of such virtue that whatever is touched by it is kept unharmed from all rot and worms. Aloe is a tree of most sweet odor, so much so that it is burned upon the altars in place of incense. But it has a most bitter juice, resisting rot and worms. Hence also in the passion of the Lord Nicodemus is said to have brought a mixture of myrrh and aloe about a hundred 324Bpounds. By these trees, then, is designated the continence and chastity of the flesh. For rot is wont to designate luxury, according to what the Prophet says of certain men: « The beasts have rotted in their dung. » In the garden of the Church, then, are myrrh and aloe, that is, heavenly minds free from all corruption, hearing from the Apostle: « Follow peace with all men, and chastity, without which no one shall see God. » « With all the chief ointments. » By the chief ointments what better is understood than charity? Of which the Apostle says: « I show you yet a more excellent way, » and then: « If I speak (he says) with the tongues of men and of angels, but have not charity, it profits me nothing. » After myrrh and aloe the chief ointments are well placed, 324Cbecause after the continence of the flesh there succeeds true charity, which is God, because God is loved above all things. For neither can those who are held by worldly love, that is, who still delight in pleasures and enticements, be partakers of this charity.

« Fountain of the gardens, well of living waters, which flow with force from Lebanon. » The fountain of the gardens is that same primitive Church, which with the knowledge of truth waters the gardens, that is, the Churches disseminated throughout the whole world; she is also the well of living waters, because the knowledge of truth, which is the Church, is in some things as it were a fountain where it lies open, in some as it were a well where it lies hidden, and beyond reach where it cannot be perceived. For between a fountain and a well this is the difference: that a fountain is said to be both in the depth and on the surface of the earth, 324Dbut a well is always in the depth. The Church, then, is a fountain and a well, because the mysteries of God in some things are easily grasped as though from a fountain, but in some are drawn out with difficulty as though waters from a well. By the living waters are designated the very mysteries of the Scriptures, because they have in themselves a heavenly power. But over against them, cisterns are named, that is, gatherings of waters, the documents of the heretics, of which the Lord says through the Prophet: « They have forsaken me, the fountain of living water, and have dug for themselves cisterns, broken cisterns, that can hold no waters. » These flowing waters flow with force from Lebanon, that is, they issue from the Church, which is washed and made white by baptism and by good works, which is what Lebanon is interpreted to mean. From this Lebanon 325A(that is, from the Church) flow (that is, issue) the waters of heavenly doctrines, and this with force, that is, with a certain power, by which all the fictions of the heretics are destroyed and overturned. Hence also the Psalmist: « The force of the river maketh glad the city of God, » that is, the power of the divine utterances.

« Arise, O north wind, and come, O south wind, blow through my garden, and let its aromatics flow. » The virtues of the Church having been enumerated under the name of aromatics, the Lord, who is the bridegroom and redeemer of the Church, knowing that the Church herself is to be multiplied by persecutions, thereupon in a certain manner bids that persecution come, not by commanding, but by permitting. « Arise (he says), O north wind, and come, O south wind. » By the north wind and the south wind are understood the blasts of persecutions and disturbances, 325Braging against the Church. For the north wind is the coldest wind, the south wind warm. And therefore by the north wind are designated terrors and threats, by the south wind the snares of flatteries. By which two ways the Church is tested, namely by terrors and by flatteries. But that he says: « Arise, O north wind, and come, O south wind, » he does not command the wicked, nor compel them to do evils, but permits, and gives them the power, that they may be able to rage against the Church, in order that through their malice the Church may be tested, and they may be more grievously punished; for the Lord knows how to work certain goods out of the evils of men, but he keeps the persecution itself in his own power, so that they may not rage as much as they will. Whence it is written: « Who maketh a weight for the winds, » because, namely, he sets a certain measure to the blasts of persecutions, 325Caccording to what the Apostle says: « He will make with the temptation also a way out, that you may be able to bear it. » « Blow through (he says) my garden, » that is, my Church, « and let its aromatics flow, » that is, let the fragrance of the virtues and the odor of good works proceed from it. For the more bitterly the Church is shaken, the greater an odor of virtues she sends forth from herself.

CAPUT V.

« Veniat dilectus meus in hortum suum, ut comedat fructum pomorum suorum. » Audiens Ecclesia persecutionem vocari, tanquam se praeparans contra adversa, dicit: « Veniat dilectus meus in hortum suum, » hoc est, Christus sponsus meus veniat, quem toto corde diligo, ad Ecclesiam suam, ut comedat 325Dfructum pomorum suorum, hoc est, delectetur atque pascatur bonis operibus fidelium suorum. Nam cibus Domini bona nostra opera sunt, sicut ipse fidem Samaritanorum vel gentium intuens, dixit discipulis suis: « Ego cibum habeo manducare quem vos nescitis. » « Veni in hortum meum, soror mea sponsa. » Desideranti Ecclesiae ut ad se veniat sponsus, respondet se jam hoc fecisse. Ego jam veni, nam non est imperantis « veni, » sed praeteriti temporis indicativum. O soror mea sponsa, jam veni in hortum meum, jam visitavi Ecclesiam meam, sed et quotidie visito, et virtutum illius pomis pascor, atque odore fruor. Venio ad eam, ut errantes corrigam, infirmantes roborem, dubios confirmem, et 326Aperfectos quoque praemiis coelestibus donem. « Messui myrrham meam cum aromatibus meis. » Per myrrham designantur illi qui martyrio vitam finierunt, vel etiam carnem suam cum vitiis et concupiscentiis crucifixerunt. Aromata vero sunt opera sanctarum virtutum, bonae opinionis odoribus fragrantia. Metit ergo Dominus myrrham cum aromatibus suis, quando sanctos martyres, vel reliquos electos bonis operibus insignes, ab hac vita falce mortis praecidit, atque ad maturitatem bonorum operum perductos, in horrea supernae patriae recondit. « Comedi favum meum cum melle meo, bibi vinum meum cum lacte meo. » Per favum et vinum sancti praedicatores figurantur; per mel vero et lac, boni auditores. Favus enim sunt praedicatores, qui 326Barcana et intima Scripturarum mysteria quasi mella de favis producunt, et praedicando aliis manifestant. Mel vero boni auditores, quia dulcedinem verbi Dei attente suscipere eaque delectabiliter pasci student. Vinum sunt ipsi praedicatores, quod et favus, quia fortia sacramenta Scripturarum annuntiant. Lac vero sunt infirmi quique auditores, quibus aperta mysteria quasi lac conveniunt. Cum ergo horum omnium bona intentione Redemptor pascitur et delectatur, et sic quasi favum cum melle comedit, et vinum cum lacte bibit. Possumus hoc etiam ad mortem electorum referre, quos Dominus comedit, cum ad aeternam vitam per mortem vocat, et corpori suo (hoc est, societati electorum in illa jam coelesti felicitate laetantium) conjungit. Quod si hoc de morte 326Csanctorum accipimus, debemus in vino eos accipere, quorum animae jam coelesti felicitate gloriantur: in favo vero eos qui jam corpore et anima in illa beatitudine gaudent, quales sunt illi qui cum Domino surrexerunt. « Comedite, amici, et bibite, et inebriamini, charissimi. » Non solum Dominus profectu sanctorum gaudet, verum etiam nos hortatur ut illorum virtutibus gaudeamus et illos imitemur: « Comedite (inquiens), amici, » hoc est, fideles mei, qui amici mei estis faciendo quae jubeo, charissimi mei estis, perfecta me animi charitate diligendo. Comedite ergo, inquit, et bibite, id est sanctorum bonis actibus congaudete, et illos vobis ad imitandum proponite. Notandum vero quod dicit, « Bibite et inebriamini; » Ebrietas aliquando in Scripturis pro 326Dsaturitate ponitur, sicut de fratribus Joseph dictum est: « Biberunt et inebriati sunt coram eo, » et Psalmista: « Visitasti terram, et inebriasti eam, » id est, satiasti et replesti. Hortatur ergo nos ut non solum comedamus et bibamus, verum etiam saturemur, quia sunt quidam qui comedunt et non saturantur, quia videlicet delectabiliter praedicationem divinam audiunt, sed negligenter obliviscuntur, nec ad effectum boni operis quod audierint perducunt. Comedunt autem et saturantur qui verba vitae quae audiunt, in internis sensibus recondunt, et opere implere satagunt; de quibus Psalmista dicit: « Edent pauperes et saturabuntur. » Si vero (ut superius dictum est) de morte sanctorum hoc acceperimus, 327Aamici et charissimi ipsi sunt angelici spiritus, quos jubet Dominus congratulari electis suis, cum ab hac vita ad aeternae beatitudinis requiem transferuntur, juxta quod in evangelica parabola legitur: « Congratulamini mihi, quia inveni ovem meam quae perierat. »

« Ego dormio, et cor meum vigilat. » Vox Ecclesiae, « Ego dormio, » quia aliquantula pace largiente sponso meo requiesco, nec tales patior pressuras, quales primitiva Ecclesia; et ideo cor meum vigilat, quia securius concessa mihi pace amori sponsi mei inhaereo, et ad illum oculos cordis mei intendo. Sed quia hoc tempus non est requiei, sed potius laboris et certaminis, rursus sponsus Ecclesiam ad labores excitat, et ad praedicationis certamen hortatur. 327B« Vox dilecti mei pulsantis. » Pulsat dilectus, cum Christus fideles suos ad profectum virtutum hortatur: sive pulsat, cum eos ut proximos praedicando lucrentur admonet. Est et tertia pulsatio, qua pulsat electos suos Dominus, cum per aegritudinis molestiam esse mortem vicinam designat. De qua pulsatione in Evangelio dicitur: Ut cum venerit et pulsaverit, confestim aperiant ei. Sed hoc loco pulsare dicitur hoc est, Ecclesiam ad opus praedicationis instigare. « Aperi mihi, soror mea, amica mea, columba mea, immaculata mea. » « Aperi mihi, soror mea. » Soror, quia cohaeres es regni mei facta: « amica mea, » quia de jugo servitutis liberata arcana veritatis meae cognovisti: « columba mea, » quia spiritus mei dote es sanctificata: « immaculata mea, » quia 327Ceffusione mei sanguinis ab omni peccatorum macula purgata es. « Aperi mihi, » hoc est, de quiete et otio dilectae tibi contemplationis egredere ad opus praedicationis. « Quia caput meum plenum est rore, et cincinni mei guttis noctium. » Caput Ecclesiae (ut Apostolus dicit) Christus est, cincinni vero sunt intimae cogitationes sanctorum, quae non laxe et dissolute fluunt, sed vinculo timoris et amoris Dei colligantur. Ros vero et guttae noctium hoc loco tenebrosas et infidelitate plenas, frigidasque mentes designant. Caput ergo sponsi plenum est rore, cum saeculares quique a charitate Dei torpescunt, et juxta quod Dominus dicit, abundante peccato refrigescit charitas. Et cum tales sanctos Dei coelestia meditantes persequuntur et odiunt, quasi cincinni sponsi pleni sunt 327Dguttis noctium. Cum ergo tales multiplicantur, et gravant Ecclesiam, tunc sponsus admonet sponsam suam ut surgat et operi praedicationis insistat.

« Exspoliavi me tunica mea, quomodo induar illa? » Provocata Ecclesia a sponso suo ad officium praedicationis, respondet: « Exspoliavi me tunica mea, quomodo induar illa? » Exui me curis et occupationibus hujus saeculi, sine quibus officium praedicationis aut vix aut nullatenus agitur: et quomodo fieri potest ut iterum ad ea quae deserui revertar? Nam qui se ad officium praedicationis adaptat suscipiendum, debet quoque temporalia subditis providere, quae sine magna sollicitudine agi non possunt. Quod autem tunica sollicitudines et curas significet, 328ADominus ostendit in Evangelio: « Et qui in tecto est, non descendat tollere tunicam suam. » Quod est aperte dicere: Qui in sublimi contemplationis arce consistit, non descendat ut tollat tunicam suam, id est ut iterum occupationibus saeculi implicetur. Timet ergo Ecclesia, vel anima quaeque sancta, hac tunica exui, et in contemplatione sui Conditoris requiescens, iterum reindui et saecularibus negotiis occupari. « Lavi pedes meos, quomodo inquinabo illos? » Lavi pedes meos, hoc est, actiones quibus nunquam pulverem tangere, hoc est, terrena agere consueveram, dignis poenitentiae fletibus ablui, adeo ut nihil nunc nisi divina me libeat cogitare. « Quomodo inquinabo illos? » id est, quomodo fieri potest, ut iterum ad cogitanda caduca et terrena 328Bredeam quae nunc ex occupato et libero corde, divino fruor contuitu?

« Dilectus meus misit manum suam per foramen, et venter meus intremuit ad tactum ejus. » Dilectus manum per foramen mittit, et ventrem tangit, cum interna conditor inspiratione cor visitat, et ad profectum virtutum accendit, seu etiam cum nos ad memoriam operum suorum revocat, ut cogitemus quia cum Deus esset, pro nobis homo fieri dignatus est, ut terrena nostra suscipiens, coelestes nos faceret, et moriendo pro nobis, vitam aeternam nobis donaret. Quod cum fit, venter intremuit, quia cum talia cogitare incipimus, cordis nostri arcana se concutiunt, dum stupere incipimus, quanta dignatione haec Conditor pro nobis agere dignatus est. Nam 328Cquod venter cor significet, ostendit Propheta, dicens: « Ventrem meum, ventrem meum doleo. » Quod quid esset ostendit, dicens: « Sensus cordis mei dissipati sunt. »

« Surrexi ut aperirem dilecto meo. » Aperimus dilecto, non solum cum adventum ejus suscipimus, verum etiam cum aliis praedicamus, et eos qui per malitiam pectus clauserant, nostra praedicatione conversos, facimus ut ipsi quoque januam cordis Christo aperiant. Bene autem primo dicitur, « Surrexi, » deinde, « ut aperirem dilecto. » Qui enim praedicando aliorum corda Christo vult aperire, prius debet surgere, hoc est ad studium bonorum operum erigi, et opere implere quod praedicat, ne forte aliis praedicans ipse reprobus efficiatur. Hinc et Lucas de 328DDomino dicit: « Quae coepit Jesus facere et docere. » Prius dicit « facere, deinde docere. » « Manus meae distillaverunt myrrham, et digiti mei pleni myrrha probatissima. » Per manus quibus operamur, ipsa operatio designatur; per digitos vero discretio operationis, quia in nullo membro tantae sunt distinctiones articulorum, quantae in digitis. Manus Ecclesiae myrrham stillant, cum praedicatores ejus continentiae et mortificationi carnis suae operam dant, dicentes cum Apostolo: « Castigo corpus meum, et in servitutem redigo. » Sive cum pro Christo mori parati sunt, sicut Joannes dicit: « Si Christus pro nobis animam suam posuit, et nos debemus pro fratribus animas ponere. » Digiti vero 329Asunt pleni myrrha probatissima, cum ipsum opus mortificationis subtiliter discernimus, utrum pro intuitu supernae mercedis fiat, an pro laudibus saeculi. Qui enim pro humanis favoribus jejunat, vel abstinentiae sive continentiae operibus insistit, in manibus quidem myrrham habet, sed in digitis non habet, quia non discernit quare hoc faciat. De qualibus Dominus dicit: « Receperunt mercedem suam. » Sive manus sponsae distillant myrrham, cum operibus sanctae Ecclesiae, castimoniae vel sanctae continentiae seipsos aptant.

« Pessulum ostii mei aperui dilecto meo, at ille declinaverat atque transierat. » Pessulum ostii aperit, cum Ecclesia vel fidelis quaeque anima cor advenienti Conditori pandit. « At ille declinaverat atque 329Btransierat. » Plerumque enim cum spiritualia quaeque tractare volumus, quanto subtilius intendimus, tanto acies cordis reverberatur. Unde Salomon dicit: « Dixi, sapiens efficiar, et ipsa longius recessit a me. » Psalmista quoque dicit: « Accedet homo ad cor altum, et exaltabitur Deus. » Quanto enim quisque magis ad contemplationem divinam purificatione carnis cor sustollit, tanto altius quod quaerebat esse invenit. Dicit ergo: « Ille declinaverat atque transierat, » quia nemo eum in praesenti vita sicuti est comprehendere potest. « Anima mea liquefacta est, ut dilectus locutus est. » Quanto (inquit) vicinius adventum sponsi mei persensi ad tactum secretae inspirationis, tanto magis quidquid in me erat frigidum, incaluit, ita ut nihil jam libeat 329Cnisi in lacrymas resolvi. Loquitur enim sponsus, cum interna sui inspiratione Christi sanctam animam inspirat, eique coeleste desiderium suggerit, et ita eo loquente liquefit, quia quo magis coelestibus afflatur desideriis, eo amplius terrenis emoritur, et quasi insensibilis mundo efficitur, solumque coelesti desiderio vivit. Hoc est quod etiam per prophetam dicitur: Quis caecus nisi servus meus? Quis surdus, nisi ad quem nuntios meos misi? « Quaesivi, et non inveni illum. Vocavi, et non respondit mihi. » Ingeminat iterum causam doloris sui. Omnibus quidem se pie quaerentibus adest Dominus semper, se invocantibus respondet, id est ad salutem exaudit, sed plerumque fidelem animam se desiderantem, et ad se venire cupientem non exaudit, 329Dad hoc ut ita se ei in praesenti ostendat, sicut se in futurum ostensurum promittit, vel etiam non respondet, id est non exaudit, ad hoc ut libito suo sanctus quisque vinculis corporis exuatur, et liber amplexibus sponsi sui demulceatur. Nam quando Apostolus dicebat: « Cupio dissolvi et esse cum Christo, » quasi quaerebat inhaerere sponso suo, et vocabat illum desiderio mentis, sed tamen non respondebat illi, quia non statim de corpore eum liberabat, sed adhuc laboribus exerceri, et utilitatibus Ecclesiae insudare permittebat.

« Invenerunt me custodes qui circumeunt civitatem, percusserunt me, et vulneraverunt me. » Vigiles qui custodiunt civitatem, sancti sunt doctores, 330Aqui civitatem Dei (id est Ecclesiam) circumeunt, pervigilem ejus curam agentes, quo illam et ab adversariis tueantur, et verbo suo vel exemplo ad coelestia desideria accendant. Inveniunt ergo sponsam dilectum quaerentem, cum sancti doctores animam coelestibus desideriis intentam, vel praesentes verbo, vel absentes Scripturis suis instituunt et informant. « Percusserunt, inquit, et vulneraverunt me. » Verbum Dei gladius est, dicente Apostolo: « Vivus est sermo Dei et efficax, et penetrabilior omni gladio ancipiti, et pertingens usque ad divisionem animae ac spiritus. » Hoc gladio sponsa percutitur et vulneratur, quando praedicatoris sermo de Deo loquitur, et auditores veluti quodam spiculo compunctionis et amoris transfodit. « Tulerunt pallium 330Bmeum mihi. » Hoc hic pallium, quod superius tunica intelligitur, ubi dicitur: « Exspoliavi me tunica mea, » tegmine videlicet saecularium desideriorum: quod custodes auferunt, quia quidquid de amore praesentis saeculi in mente alicujus remanserit, totum sancti doctores auferunt, et eam solummodo sponsum Christum diligere faciunt. Hi tales possunt dicere cum Apostolo: « Mihi mundus crucifixus est, et ego mundo. » « Custodes murorum. » Muri civitatis sunt sancti doctores Ecclesiae. Custodes autem murorum illi sunt qui etiam eos instruunt, qui aliis quoque praedicare possunt, qualis erat Paulus, qui Timotheum, Titum, muros utique hujus civitatis, instruebat et custodiebat, hoc est exhortabatur ad perfectionem boni operis.

330C« Adjuro vos, filiae Hierusalem, si inveneritis dilectum meum, ut nuntietis ei quia amore langueo. » Filiae Hierusalem sunt animae fideles, quae vel adhuc in praesenti peregrinantur, vel etiam Christo fruuntur. Filiae Hierusalem, id est filiae illius supernae civitatis in qua est summa pax et felicitas divinae contemplationis. Sed hoc loco filias Hierusalem animas debemus accipere Deum desiderantes, sed adhuc in hac peregrinatione versantes. Adjurat ergo sponsa filias Hierusalem, ut nuntient sponso quia amore languet, cum anima fidelis fideles alios Deum desiderantes adjurat, ut Christo insinuent, eique nuntient suo illam amore teneri. Bene autem se amore languere dicit: quanto enim magis superna 330Ddesiderare coeperit, tanto erga ea quae mundi sunt, languescit. Nec mirum si tales languere dicuntur, cum Apostolus eos mortuos dicat: « Mortui (inquiens) estis, et vita vestra abscondita est cum Christo. » Ita ergo adjuranti sponsae respondent filiae Hierusalem.

« Qualis est dilectus tuus ex dilecto, o pulcherrima mulierum? qualis est dilectus tuus ex dilecto, quia sic adjurasti nos? » Hoc tunc fit quando quis fidelis fratrem fidelem alloquitur, et invicem sibi coelestium desideriorum verba suggerunt, quibus se ad supernorum amorem accendunt. Indica mihi, inquit, jam desideranti, jam ad visionem Conditoris mei pervenire cupienti, qualis est dilectus tuus, hoc 331Aest qualiter Christus amari debeat. Jam dudum, inquit, illum timere coepi, sed jam charitate timorem expellente, opto audire verba quibus ad amorem illius accendar. Tu ergo quae illum jam amore quaeris, ejusque amore langues, dic mihi qualis est dilectus tuus ex dilecto. Dilectus ex dilecto, Filius est ex Patre, sicut est Deus de Deo, lumen de lumine. Vel etiam « dilectus tuus ex dilecto, » Christus est ex ea parte, qua diligendus est, non ex ea qua timendus. Nam timere magis imperfectorum est, quos timor a peccatis revocat, amare vero perfectorum. Jam ergo interrogat qualis sit dilectus ex dilecto, id est qualiter Christus amari debeat, vel qualis sit erga illos qui illum tantum amare noverunt.

331B« Dilectus meus candidus et rubicundus, electus ex millibus. » Vox respondentis Ecclesiae: « Dilectus meus candidus et rubicundus: » candidus virginitate, rubicundus passione. Candidus, quia sine peccato; nam sine peccato est conversatus: « peccatum enim non fecit, nec inventus est dolus in ore ejus. » « Rubicundus, » quia « lavit nos a peccatis nostris in sanguine suo. « Electus ex millibus. » Singulari enim gratia homo Christus in omni humani generis massa refulsit, quia per illum proposuit Deus salvare genus humanum, et ipse est mediator Dei et hominum Jesus Christus; de quo Psalmista: « Non est qui faciat bonum, non est usque ad unum. » Et Salomon: « Virum de mille unum reperi, » id est Christum, et ipse in psalmo: 331C« Singulariter sum ego donec transeam. » « Electus ergo ex millibus, » quia ex omni multitudine sanctorum solus dignus fuit audire: « Hic est filius meus dilectus, in quo mihi complacui. »

« Caput ejus aurum optimum. » Caput sponsi Deus est, juxta quod Apostolus dicit, « Caput Christi Deus, » quod auro optimo comparatur, quia sicut aurum omnibus metallis pretiosius est, ita Deus omnipotens a se factis bonis praecellit et supereminet. Unde Dominus: « Nemo bonus nisi solus Deus. » Caput ergo sponsi aurum optimum est, quia Dei bonitas est ineffabilis, omnium rerum bonitatem transcendens, quia nihil bonum, nisi participatione hujus summi boni. « Comae ejus sicut elatae palmarum. » Comae sponsi sunt multitudines fidelium, per 331Dfidem et dilectionem Deo adhaerentes, quae elatis palmarum comparantur. Elatae palmarum sunt rami productiores et excellentiores, interdum aureosi soloris, crispantes, et semper ad excelsa tendentes, et nunquam virorem suum amittentes. Tales ergo dicit comas sponsi sui Ecclesia, crispas videlicet et rutilas. Nonnulli dicunt elatas palmarum, speciem arboris esse aromaticae, quam Latine abietem sive spatas vocant. Nam Graece abies ἐλάτη dicitur. Est autem species haec apta conficiendis unguentis. Abietem vero hic non magnam illam arborem, quae est navigiis et domibus apta, sed (ut diximus) speciem aromaticae arboris debemus accipere: haec juxta itteram de elatis palmarum Comae ergo sponsi 332Aelatis palmarum comparantur, quia fidelium multitudines et virore fidei gaudent, et ad aeterna desideranda extolluntur, atque dulcedinem supernae suavitatis degustant, quam quanto magis percipiunt, tanto magis in oculis suis peccatores sibi et contemptibiles esse videntur. Unde sequitur: « Nigrae quasi corvus. » Comae enim sponsi nigrae sunt quasi corvus, quia fidelium multitudines suae fragilitatis sibi consciae sunt, et nihil se boni ex se habere noverunt. Vel etiam « elatae palmarum » sunt in spe et victoria: « nigrae vero quasi corvus, » quasi despecti et contemptibiles sunt pressuris hujus saeculi. Elatae palmarum sunt sancti, quia per gratiam Dei ad coelestis victoriae palmam tendunt; nigrae vero quasi corvus, quia per se infirmos et peccatores 332Bse esse cognoscunt. Possunt etiam comae sponsi angelicae virtutes accipi, quae Deo specialiter inhaerent, sicut comae capiti, eumque a vicino contemplantur. Quae bene elatis comparantur, quia nunquam a status sui rectitudine inclinantur, sed in dignitate officii sui immaculate permanent, quae quanto magis claritatem sui conditoris contemplantur, eo amplius quam utile sit omne creatum, in ejus comparatione aspiciunt, et ideo nigrae quasi corvus esse dicuntur.

« Oculi ejus sicut columbae super rivulos aquarum, quae lacte sunt lotae. » Oculi ejus dona sunt Spiritus sancti. Quod ostendit Joannes in Apocalypsi, dicens: « Vidi agnum tanquam occisum, habentem cornua septem et oculos septem, qui sunt septem 332Cspiritus Dei missi in omnem terram. » Unde et bene columbis super rivulos aquarum comparantur, quia Spiritus sanctus puris et sinceris mentibus delectatur. Non enim super stagna vel paludes, sed super rivulos aquarum columbae resident, quia (ut dictum est) Spiritus sanctus castas et mundas mentes sibi habitaculum facit. « Quae lacte sunt lotae, » quia (ut dictum est) Spiritus sanctus lactis nomine, id est Dei gratia intelligitur, quae per Spiritum sanctum Ecclesiae tribuitur, et qua veluti lacte parvuli, ita Ecclesia nutritur. Possumus per oculos sponsi doctores accipere, qui columbis comparantur propter innocentiam et simplicitatem. Hae columbae super rivulos resident, ut adventum accipitris a longe prospiciant et praecaveant, sic et sancti doctores aqua 332Dbaptismatis perfusi, nitore castitatis et sanctimoniae dealbati sunt, « et resident juxta fluenta plenissima » hoc est juxta Scripturarum exuberantem scientiam. Si quid vero putamus distare inter rivulos et fluenta plenissima, dicendum est, per rivulos Veteris Testamenti Scripturas designari: per fluenta plenissima, copiosam sancti Evangelii Scripturam accipi. Istae ergo columbae et super rivulos aquarum sunt, et juxta fluenta plenissima resident, quia sancti doctores assidue tam Veteris Vestamenti quam Novi mysteria perscrutantur.

« Genae illius sicut areolae aromatum, consitae a pigmentariis. » Genae sponsi, modesta Christi pietas, vel severitas, sive habitus vultus illius accipitur. 333AGenae enim illius erant, cum exsultaret in spiritu, ut Evangelium dicit, vel cum fleret super mortuum Lazarum, vel cum doleret super perituram Hierusalem, et plura id genus. Hae genae sponsi areolis aromatum comparantur sicut enim areolae aromatum optime compositae et ordinatae aspectum intuentium delectant, eisque odoris sui gratiam propinant, ita Christi mansuetudo et absentes fama, et praesentes re ipsa delectabat, et ad sui dilectionem provocabat. Hae areolae a pigmentariis consitae sunt. Pigmentarii sunt prophetae vel apostoli, quorum alteri haec omnia futura, alteri jam facta descripserunt. « Labia illius lilia distillantia myrrham primam. » Per labia sponsi, Verba Domini nostri accipiuntur, quae liliis comparantur, quia candoris aeterni praemia annuntiant: 333Bdistillant myrrham primam, quia per carnis mortificationem ad haec perveniendum docent. Vel etiam liliis labia Domini comparantur, quia castitatis illa et munditiae opera docent. Distillant primam myrrham, quia quaecunque adversa patienter perferenda insinuant. Labia Domini lilia erant, cum docens in monte diceret: « Beati pauperes spiritu, beati mites, beati misericordes, beati mundo corde. » Distillant myrrham primam, cum post haec subjungeret: « Beati qui persecutionem patiuntur propter justitiam. »

« Manus ejus tornatiles aureae. » In manibus namque sponsi, digne operatio Salvatoris accipitur. Sicut enim quis torno facile operatur, nec regula ulla indiget, sic operatio Salvatoris facilis erat: quidquid 333Cenim volebat, illico perficiebat, quia « dixit et facta sunt; » et de quo Evangelista, « Erat quasi in potestate habens sermonem. » Aureae enim erant, quia operatio illius divinitatis peragebatur potentia: nam in auro divinitas figuratur, quia sicut aurum caetera metalla antecellit, et pretiosius est, ita divinitas suis omnibus operibus ineffabili modo praecellit. « Plenae hyacinthis. » Hyacinthus lapis est aerii coloris. Quid ergo in hyacinthis, nisi spes et desiderium coeleste accipitur? Et manus Domini plenae sunt hyacinthis, quia omnis ejus operatio nos ad spem et desiderium supernorum excitat. Hyacinthus est etiam flos aromaticus, coloris purpurei bonique odoris. Manus Domini plenae sunt hyacinthis, quia in cruce clavis perforatae, et rubore sui sanguinis quasi 333Dcolore purpureo respersae sunt. Sed superior expositio de hyacintho lapide, melius hic convenit. In alia translatione « lapis » habetur. Dicuntur enim manus ejus tornatiles, plenae tharsis. Tharsis lapis est quem nos chalcedonium dicimus. « Venter ejus eburneus. » In membris humani corporis nihil ventre fragilius, nihil tenerius: ideoque per ventrem fragilitas assumptae humanitatis in Christo accipitur. Ebur autem est os elephantis, quod animal dicunt esse castissimum, et naturae frigidissimae: unde et a dracone, qui calidae naturae est, vehementer appetitur. Venter ergo sponsi eburneus est, quia assumpta humanitas Redemptoris, nullam corruptionem, nullam labem admisit. Peccatum enim non 334Afecit, nec dolus inventus est in eo ullus. Fragilitatem quidem carnis assumpsit, sed tamen totius peccati immunis fuit, sicut serpens aeneus a Moyse in deserto exaltatus, figuram quidem serpentis habuit, sed malitia veneni caruit. « Distinctus sapphiris. » Sapphirus lapis est, coeli sereni habens colorem, unde in visione Domini dicitur: « Et viderunt Deum Israel, sub pedibus ejus quasi opus lapidis sapphiri, et quasi coelum cum serenum est. » Per sapphiros ergo opera divinae majestatis intelliguntur, quae in carne perficiuntur. Venter ergo sponsi distinctus erat sapphiris, quia humanitas Christi coelestibus virtutibus refulgebat. Nec plenus dicitur esse sapphiris, sed « distinctus, » ita videlicet, ut inter sapphiros appareret eboris candor, quia sic Dominus 334Boperabatur ea quae divinitatis erant, ut nihilominus perficeret ea quae erant humanitatis, et non relinqueret ea quae erant divinitatis. Distincta est enim operatio in Christo divinitatis et humanitatis. Nam quod esuriebat, quod sitiebat, quod flebat, quod lassabatur, quod ad ultimum crucifigi et mori poterat, humanitatis opera erant: quod vero mortuos suscitabat, quod omnibus infirmantibus succurrebat, quod seipsum a mortuis resuscitabat, evidentissima erant opera divinitatis.

« Crura illius columnae marmoreae, quae fundatae sunt super bases aureas. » Crura sponsi itinera sunt Salvatoris, quibus homo fieri et ad nos venire dignatus est. Quae recte columnis marmoreis comparantur, quia nihil marmore fortius, nihil columna rectius. 334CEt crura sponsi columnae marmoreae sunt, quia omnia itinera Domini et fortia et recta sunt. De ejus fortitudine dicit Psalmista: « Exsultavit ut gigas ad currendam viam, a summo coelo egressio ejus. » De rectitudine alibi: « Justus Dominus et justitias dilexit, aequitatem vidit vultus ejus; » et, « Universae viae Domini misericordia et veritas. » Haec crura super bases aureas fundata sunt, quia omnia quae per Christum vel in Christo agenda erant, divinitatis consilio ante mundi constitutionem praeordinata et praefinita sunt. Aurum enim quod (ut saepe dictum est) splendidius et pretiosius est caeteris metallis, divini consilii sincerissimum secretum significat. De basibus enim aureis columnae marmoreae procedunt, quia ab initio saeculi praeordinata est incarnatio, nativitas, 334Dpassio, et resurrectio Salvatoris, caeteraque mysteria quibus humanum genus redimeretur. Unde et Propheta laudans Deum dicebat: Domine Deus meus, honorificabo te, laudes dicam nomini tuo qui facis mirabiles res: consilium tuum antiquum verum fiat. « Species ejus ut Libani, electus ut cedri. » Libanus Phoenicis est mons, terminus Judeae contra septentrionem, et est excelsior caeteris montibus. Arbores illius montis et proceritate et specie ac robore caeterarum sylvarum ligna praecellunt. Sicut (inquit) Libanus caeteris montibus Judaeae terrae praecelsior est, ita Redemptor noster speciosior est omnibus electis, quia speciosus est forma prae filiis hominum. Et sicut pulchrior est et procerior caeteris 335Aarboribus, ita et ille gratia divinitatis praecellit omnes ab initio saeculi sanctos.

« Guttur illius suavissimum. » Superius per labia sponsi, verba ejus intellecta sunt, nunc per guttur ipsorum verborum interior dulcedo significatur, qua intellectus noster reficitur. Unde et Psalmista: « Gustate et videte quoniam suavis est Dominus. » Et multi quidem verba Domini facile intelligere possunt, sed pauci sunt qui dulcedinem illorum valeant penetrare; hoc est qui praecepta Dei ex amore et desiderio studeant adimplere. « Et totus desiderabilis. » Ac si diceret: Quid per singula membra ejus describendo immoror? Totus est desiderabilis sponsus meus. Totus, inquit, quia perfectus Deus, perfectus 335Bhomo. Totus ergo desiderabilis est, quia angelos et electos quosque ad suum accendit desiderium, juxta quod Apostolus dicit: « In quem desiderant angeli prospicere, » et quo vicinius accipitur, hoc majori desiderio ad se aspiciendum provocat intuentes. « Talis est dilectus meus, et iste est amicus meus, filiae Hierusalem. » Dilectus, quia per fidem et charitatem illi inhaereo: amicus vero, quia me de vinculo peccati redemptam, amicam suam et secretorum suorum consciam facere dignatus est. Quem quanto plus quisque diligit, tanto amicitia ejus dignior efficitur.

« Quo abiit dilectus tuus, o pulcherrima mulierum? » Vox est filiarum Hierusalem, id est Ecclesiarum, sive etiam animarum sanctarum. Nam 335Csponsa Christi Ecclesia est, filiae Hierusalem ipsa Ecclesia est, quae ex singulis fidelium animabus constat. Aiunt ergo: « Quo abiit dilectus tuus, o pulcherrima mulierum? Quo declinavit dilectus tuus? et quaeremus eum tecum. » Adjuraverat enim superius ipsa sponsa filias Hierusalem, ut nuntiarent sponso quod amore ejus langueret; interrogaverant eaedem filiae Hierusalem, qualis esset sponsus, et illa consequenter formam sponsi sui exposuerat; nunc interrogant ipsae filiae Hierusalem quo abierit sponsus ille, ut quaerant eum cum ea: hoc faciunt sanctae animae, quando ad invicem de aeterna salute colloquuntur, flagrantes amore Christi, et ejus aspectum desiderantes; sive eum diligenter investigant, in quorum maxime cordibus Christus requiescit, ut 335Deorum exemplo et conversatione aedificentur. Notandum autem quod dicitur, Quo abiit, vel « quo declinavit dilectus tuus? » Quasi enim ad tempus declinat dum quaeritur, ut majus ad se quaerendum desiderium excitet. Ad hoc enim plerumque differt vota et orationes sanctorum, ut ardentius eum desiderare incipiant, sicut et quondam Mariae se quaerenti non statim se ostendit, sed in specie hortulani apparens, tandem quod ipse qui quaerebatur esset, ostendit. Exaudit ergo Christus semper electos suos, sed semper quasi declinat, cum non statim postulata concedit. Bene autem dicitur, « quaeremus tecum, » nam qui sine Ecclesia Christum quaerit, errare et fatigari potest, proficere non potest.

CHAPTER V.

« Let my beloved come into his garden, that he may eat the fruit of his orchard. » The Church, hearing persecution called for, as though preparing herself against adversities, says: « Let my beloved come into his garden, » that is, let Christ my bridegroom, whom I love with my whole heart, come to his Church, that he may eat 325Dthe fruit of his orchard, that is, that he may be delighted and fed with the good works of his faithful. For the food of the Lord is our good works, as he himself, beholding the faith of the Samaritans or of the Gentiles, said to his disciples: « I have food to eat which you know not. » « I have come into my garden, my sister, my bride. » To the Church desiring that the bridegroom come to her, he answers that he has already done this. I have already come; for it is not « come » of one commanding, but the indicative of the past tense. O my sister, my bride, I have already come into my garden, I have already visited my Church, but I also visit it daily, and am fed with the fruits of its virtues, and enjoy its odor. I come to it, that I may correct the erring, strengthen the sick, confirm the doubtful, and 326Aendow the perfect also with heavenly rewards. « I have reaped my myrrh with my aromatics. » By myrrh are designated those who ended their life in martyrdom, or also crucified their flesh with its vices and concupiscences. But the aromatics are the works of holy virtues, fragrant with the odors of good repute. The Lord, then, reaps his myrrh with his aromatics, when he cuts off from this life, by the sickle of death, the holy martyrs or the rest of the elect distinguished by good works, and, when they have been brought to the maturity of good works, stores them in the barns of the heavenly fatherland. « I have eaten my honeycomb with my honey, I have drunk my wine with my milk. » By the honeycomb and the wine the holy preachers are figured; but by the honey and the milk, the good hearers. For the honeycomb are the preachers, who 326Bbring forth the secret and inmost mysteries of the Scriptures like honey from the combs, and by preaching make them manifest to others. But the honey are the good hearers, because they strive to receive attentively the sweetness of the word of God and to be delightfully fed by it. The wine are the very preachers, as is also the honeycomb, because they announce the strong sacraments of the Scriptures. But the milk are all the weak hearers, to whom the open mysteries are suited like milk. When, then, the Redeemer is fed and delighted with the good intention of all these, and thus, as it were, eats the honeycomb with the honey, and drinks the wine with the milk. We can also refer this to the death of the elect, whom the Lord eats, when through death he calls them to eternal life, and joins them to his body (that is, to the fellowship of the elect now rejoicing in that heavenly happiness). But if we take this of the death 326Cof the saints, we ought to understand by the wine those whose souls now glory in heavenly happiness; but by the honeycomb those who now rejoice in body and soul in that beatitude, such as are they who rose with the Lord. « Eat, O friends, and drink, and be inebriated, O dearest. » Not only does the Lord rejoice in the progress of the saints, but he also exhorts us to rejoice in their virtues and to imitate them: « Eat (he says), O friends, » that is, my faithful, who are my friends by doing what I command, are my dearest, by loving me with perfect charity of soul. Eat, then, he says, and drink, that is, rejoice together in the good deeds of the saints, and set them before yourselves for imitation. But it is to be noted that he says, « Drink and be inebriated »: inebriation is sometimes put in the Scriptures for 326Dfullness, as it was said of the brethren of Joseph: « They drank and were inebriated with him, » and the Psalmist: « Thou hast visited the earth, and hast inebriated it, » that is, hast filled and replenished it. He exhorts us, then, not only to eat and drink, but also to be filled, because there are certain who eat and are not filled, because, namely, they hear the divine preaching delightfully, but negligently forget it, and do not carry to the effect of good work what they have heard. But they eat and are filled who store the words of life which they hear in their inward senses, and strive to fulfill them in deed; of whom the Psalmist says: « The poor shall eat and shall be filled. » But if (as was said above) we take this of the death of the saints, 327Athe friends and the dearest are the angelic spirits themselves, whom the Lord bids to congratulate his elect, when they are transferred from this life to the rest of eternal beatitude, according to what is read in the Gospel parable: « Congratulate me, because I have found my sheep which was lost. »

« I sleep, and my heart watcheth. » The voice of the Church, « I sleep, » because, my bridegroom granting some little peace, I am at rest, and do not suffer such oppressions as the primitive Church did; and therefore my heart watcheth, because, peace being granted me, I cling the more securely to the love of my bridegroom, and direct the eyes of my heart toward him. But because this is a time not of rest, but rather of labor and struggle, again the bridegroom rouses the Church to labors, and exhorts her to the struggle of preaching. 327B« The voice of my beloved knocking. » The beloved knocks, when Christ exhorts his faithful to the progress of the virtues; or he knocks, when he admonishes them to gain their neighbors by preaching. There is also a third knocking, by which the Lord knocks at his elect, when through the trouble of sickness he signifies that death is near. Of which knocking it is said in the Gospel: That when he comes and knocks, they may open to him at once. But in this place to knock means this, namely to incite the Church to the work of preaching. « Open to me, my sister, my love, my dove, my undefiled. » « Open to me, my sister. » Sister, because thou art made co-heir of my kingdom: « my love, » because, freed from the yoke of servitude, thou hast come to know the secrets of my truth: « my dove, » because thou art sanctified by the dowry of my Spirit: « my undefiled, » because 327Cby the shedding of my blood thou art purged from every stain of sins. « Open to me, » that is, go forth from the quiet and leisure of the contemplation dear to thee, to the work of preaching. « For my head is full of dew, and my locks of the drops of the nights. » The head of the Church (as the Apostle says) is Christ, but the locks are the inmost thoughts of the saints, which do not flow loosely and dissolutely, but are bound together by the bond of the fear and love of God. But the dew and the drops of the nights in this place designate the dark and unbelieving and cold minds. The head of the bridegroom, then, is full of dew, when all the worldly grow numb to the love of God, and, according to what the Lord says, sin abounding, charity grows cold. And when such men persecute and hate the saints of God who meditate on the heavenly things, the locks of the bridegroom are, as it were, full 327Dof the drops of the nights. When, therefore, such men are multiplied, and burden the Church, then the bridegroom admonishes his bride to arise and press on to the work of preaching.

« I have put off my tunic, how shall I put it on? » The Church, called forth by her bridegroom to the office of preaching, answers: « I have put off my tunic, how shall I put it on? » I have stripped myself of the cares and occupations of this world, without which the office of preaching is either scarcely or by no means carried out: and how can it be that I return again to those things which I forsook? For he who fits himself to undertake the office of preaching ought also to provide temporal things for his subjects, which cannot be done without great solicitude. But that the tunic signifies solicitudes and cares, 328Athe Lord shows in the Gospel: « And let him who is on the housetop not come down to take his tunic. » Which is openly to say: Let him who stands in the lofty citadel of contemplation not come down to take his tunic, that is, that he be again entangled in the occupations of the world. The Church, then, or any holy soul, fears to be stripped of this tunic, and, resting in the contemplation of her Creator, to be reclothed again and occupied with worldly affairs. « I have washed my feet, how shall I defile them? » I have washed my feet, that is, the actions with which I was never wont to touch the dust, that is, to do earthly things, I have washed with worthy weeping of penance, so much so that now nothing pleases me but to think on the divine things. « How shall I defile them? » that is, how can it be that I return again to thinking on the perishable and earthly things 328Bwhich now, with a free and unoccupied heart, I enjoy in divine contemplation?

« My beloved put his hand through the opening, and my belly trembled at his touch. » The beloved puts his hand through the opening, and touches the belly, when the Creator visits the heart with inward inspiration, and kindles it to the progress of the virtues, or also when he recalls us to the memory of his works, that we may consider that, though he was God, he deigned to become man for us, that, taking upon himself our earthly things, he might make us heavenly, and, by dying for us, might bestow eternal life upon us. And when this is done, the belly trembles, because when we begin to consider such things, the secret places of our heart are shaken, while we begin to be amazed with how great condescension the Creator deigned to do these things for us. For 328Cthat the belly signifies the heart, the Prophet shows, saying: « My belly, my belly, I am in pain. » And what this was he shows, saying: « The senses of my heart are troubled. »

« I rose up to open to my beloved. » We open to the beloved, not only when we receive his coming, but also when we preach to others, and make those who through malice had closed their breast, converted by our preaching, that they themselves also open the door of their heart to Christ. But rightly is it first said, « I rose up, » and then, « to open to my beloved. » For he who by preaching wishes to open the hearts of others to Christ ought first to rise up, that is, to be raised to the zeal of good works, and to fulfill in deed what he preaches, lest perhaps, preaching to others, he himself become reprobate. Hence also Luke says of the 328DLord: « Which Jesus began to do and to teach. First he says to do, » then « to teach. » « My hands dripped with myrrh, and my fingers were full of the most approved myrrh. » By the hands with which we work, the very working is designated; but by the fingers the discretion of the working, because in no member are there so many distinctions of joints as in the fingers. The hands of the Church drip myrrh, when its preachers give heed to the continence and mortification of their flesh, saying with the Apostle: « I chastise my body, and bring it into subjection. » Or when they are prepared to die for Christ, as John says: « If Christ laid down his life for us, we also ought to lay down our lives for the brethren. » But the fingers 329Aare full of the most approved myrrh, when we subtly discern the very work of mortification, whether it be done for the regard of the heavenly reward, or for the praises of the world. For he who fasts for human favors, or applies himself to the works of abstinence or continence, has indeed myrrh in his hands, but has it not in his fingers, because he does not discern why he does this. Of such the Lord says: « They have received their reward. » Or the hands of the bride drip myrrh, when they fit themselves to the works of the holy Church, of chastity or of holy continence.

« I opened the bolt of my door to my beloved, but he had turned aside and had passed on. » One opens the bolt of the door, when the Church, or any faithful soul, throws open the heart to the coming Creator. « But he had turned aside and had 329Bpassed on. » For very often, when we wish to treat of any spiritual things, the more subtly we strain, the more the edge of the heart is beaten back. Whence Solomon says: « I said, I will be wise, and it withdrew farther from me. » The Psalmist too says: « Man shall come to a deep heart, and God shall be exalted. » For the more anyone lifts up his heart to divine contemplation by the purification of the flesh, the higher he finds that to be which he was seeking. He says therefore: « He had turned aside and had passed on, » because no one in the present life can comprehend him as he is. « My soul melted when my beloved spoke. » The more nearly (she says) I perceived the coming of my bridegroom at the touch of his secret inspiration, the more all that was cold in me grew warm, so that now nothing pleases me 329Cbut to be dissolved into tears. For the bridegroom speaks, when by his inward inspiration he inspires the holy soul of Christ, and suggests to it heavenly desire, and thus, as he speaks, it melts, because the more it is breathed upon by heavenly desires, the more it dies to earthly things, and becomes as it were insensible to the world, and lives by heavenly desire alone. This is what is also said through the prophet: Who is blind but my servant? Who is deaf but he to whom I have sent my messengers? « I sought him, and found him not. I called, and he answered me not. » She redoubles again the cause of her sorrow. The Lord indeed is always present to all who piously seek him, he answers those who call upon him, that is, he hears them unto salvation, but very often he does not hear the faithful soul that desires him and longs to come to him, 329Dto this end, that he may so show himself to it in the present as he promises to show himself in the future; or also he answers not, that is, hears not, to this end, that at his good pleasure every holy one may be stripped of the bonds of the body, and, being free, may be caressed by the embraces of his bridegroom. For when the Apostle said: « I desire to be dissolved and to be with Christ, » it was as though he sought to cling to his bridegroom, and called him with the desire of his mind, and yet he answered him not, because he did not at once free him from the body, but still permitted him to be exercised with labors, and to sweat in the services of the Church.

« The keepers who go about the city found me, they struck me, and wounded me. » The watchmen who keep the city are the holy teachers, 330Awho go about the city of God (that is, the Church), keeping their vigilant care of it, so that they may both protect it from adversaries, and by their word or example kindle it to heavenly desires.
